Supposons que l'homomorphisme canonique $\mathcal O_X\to f_*(\mathcal O_{X'})$ soit
bijectif; alors, pour qu'un $\mathcal O_{X'}$-Module localement libre de rang fini
$\mathscr E'$ soit isomorphe à un $\mathcal O_{X'}$-Module de la forme
$f^*(\mathscr E)$, o

=================== PAGE IV-4 315 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 314: shown above in this file) -----
----- (English page IV-4 315: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 316 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{316}
(i) Since the bijectivity of the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})$ is a local property on $\mathrm{X}$, it remains only to see, by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.5}, that the following property holds. Denoting by $j_\alpha:\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{W}_\alpha\to\mathrm{W}_\alpha$ the canonical injection, if, for every $\alpha$ and every invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}$-Module $\mathscr{L}$, $(j_\alpha)_*(\mathscr{L}|(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{W}_\alpha))$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{W}_\alpha}$-Module, then $j_*(\mathscr{L})$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module.
But being an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module is local on $\mathrm{X}$, and $(\mathrm{W}_\alpha)$ is an open covering of $\mathrm{X}$; since
\[
  j_*(\mathscr{L})|\mathrm{W}_\alpha=(j_\alpha)_*(\mathscr{L}|(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{W}_\alpha)),
\]
this proves the assertion.

(ii) Set $\mathrm{U}'=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}'$ and let $j':\mathrm{U}'\to\mathrm{X}$ be the canonical injection.
To say that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j'_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}'})$ is bijective amounts to saying that, for every open $\mathrm{V}$ of $\mathrm{X}$, the homomorphism $\Gamma(\mathrm{V},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})\to\Gamma(\mathrm{V}\cap\mathrm{U}',\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})$ is bijective. But the composite
\[
  \Gamma(\mathrm{V},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})\to\Gamma(\mathrm{V}\cap\mathrm{U}',\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})\to\Gamma(\mathrm{V}\cap\mathrm{U},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})
\]
is bijective by hypothesis, and, on replacing $\mathrm{V}$ by $\mathrm{V}\cap\mathrm{U}'$, one sees that the second arrow is bijective; hence the first arrow is bijective.

Next let $j'':\mathrm{U}\to\mathrm{U}'$ be the canonical injection, and let $\mathscr{L}'$ be an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}'}$-Module. Then $\mathscr{L}'|\mathrm{U}$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}$-Module, so by hypothesis $j_*(\mathscr{L}'|\mathrm{U})=j'_*(j''_*(\mathscr{L}'|\mathrm{U}))$
is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module. Since the pair $(\mathrm{U}',\mathrm{U}'\cap\mathrm{Y})$ is parafactorial by (i), one has $j''_*(\mathscr{L}'|\mathrm{U})\cong\mathscr{L}'$, and therefore $j'_*(\mathscr{L}')$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module. To conclude, it suffices to replace $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{U}$, and $\mathrm{U}'$ in the preceding argument by $\mathrm{V}$, $\mathrm{V}\cap\mathrm{U}$, and $\mathrm{V}\cap\mathrm{U}'$, where $\mathrm{V}$ is an open subset of $\mathrm{X}$.

(iii) Set $\mathrm{U}'=\mathrm{X}'\setmin\mathrm{Y}'=f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})$ and note that, since we are dealing with preschemes, one may write $\mathrm{U}'=\mathrm{U}\times_{\mathrm{X}}\mathrm{X}'$. Let $f_{\mathrm{U}}:f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\to\mathrm{U}$ be the restriction of $f$, and let $j':\mathrm{U}'\to\mathrm{X}'$ be the canonical injection, also denoted $j_{(\mathrm{X}')}$.
Let us first show that the canonical homomorphism $\rho:\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})$ is bijective. By hypothesis, the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}'}\to j'_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}'})$ is bijective. Since $j$ is quasi-compact and separated by hypothesis, and $f$ is flat, $j'_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}'})=j'_*(f_{\mathrm{U}}^*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}))$ identifies canonically with $f^*(j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}))$ by \sref{IV.2.3.1}. Since $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}'}=f^*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})$, the homomorphism $f^*(\rho):f^*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})\to f^*(j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}))$ is therefore bijective; hence $\rho$ is bijective because $f$ is faithfully flat \sref{IV.2.2.7}.

Now let $\mathscr{L}$ be an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}$-Module, and set $\mathscr{L}'=f_{\mathrm{U}}^*(\mathscr{L})$, which is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}'}$-Module. By hypothesis, $j'_*(\mathscr{L}')=j'_*(f_{\mathrm{U}}^*(\mathscr{L}))$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}'}$-Module; as above, it is isomorphic by \sref{IV.2.3.1} to $f^*(j_*(\mathscr{L}))$. Since $f$ is faithfully flat and quasi-compact, it follows that $j_*(\mathscr{L})$ is a locally free, hence invertible, $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module \sref{IV.2.5.2}. To complete the proof, it suffices to replace $\mathrm{X}$ by an open $\mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{X}'$ by $f^{-1}(\mathrm{V})$ in the preceding argument.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[21.13.7]
\label{IV.21.13.7}
We say that a \emph{local ring} $\mathrm{A}$ is parafactorial if, setting $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ and denoting by $a$ the closed point of $\mathrm{X}$, the pair $(\mathrm{X},\{a\})$ is parafactorial.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.8]
\label{IV.21.13.8}
The notations being those of \sref{IV.21.13.7}, set $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\{a\}$.
For $\mathrm{A}$ to be parafactorial, it is necessary and sufficient that it satisfies the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  \item The canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{A}=\Gamma(\mathrm{X},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})\to\Gamma(\mathrm{U},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})$ is bijective.
  \item $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})=0$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.5]
\label{IV.21.13.5}
Soient $X$ un espace annelé, $Y$ une partie fermée de $X$, $U=X\setmin Y$,
$j:U\to X$ l'injectio

=================== PAGE IV-4 316 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 315: shown above in this file) -----

\oldpage[IV-4]{317}
If furthermore $\mathrm{A}$ is Noetherian, condition \textup{(i)} is equivalent to
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i \emph{bis})}] $\operatorname{prof}(\mathrm{A})\geq 2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, the only open of $\mathrm{X}$ containing $a$ is $\mathrm{X}$ itself, and hence every invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$, i.e.\ $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{X})=0$; the first assertion follows therefore from the definition \sref{IV.21.13.1} and from \sref{IV.21.13.3}.
The second assertion is nothing but a particular case of \sref{IV.21.13.4}.
\end{proof}

\begin{env}[21.13.9]
\label{IV.21.13.9}
\emph{Examples}. ---
(i)
A local Noetherian parafactorial ring is necessarily of dimension $\geq 2$ by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.8}; in other words a local Noetherian ring of dimension $\leq 1$ is not parafactorial.

(ii)
A local Noetherian \emph{factorial} ring $\mathrm{A}$ of dimension $\geq 2$ is parafactorial, as follows from \sref{IV.21.13.8} and \sref{IV.21.6.14}.

(iii)
If $\mathrm{A}$ is a local Noetherian ring of dimension $\geq 3$ and parafactorial, it is not necessarily normal nor even reduced.
Consider a regular local ring $\mathrm{B}$ of dimension $\geq 3$, and let $\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{B}}(\mathrm{B})$ $(\mathbf{0}$, $18.2.3)$, isomorphic to $\mathrm{B}[\mathrm{T}]/(\mathrm{T}^2)$; we see immediately that $\operatorname{prof}(\mathrm{A})=\operatorname{prof}(\mathrm{B})\geq 3$.
To show that $\mathrm{A}$ is parafactorial, it suffices therefore, with the notations of \sref{IV.21.13.8}, to prove that $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})=0$.
Let $\mathfrak{J}$ be the ideal of the augmentation $\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}$, which is such that $\mathfrak{J}^2=0$ and which, as a $\mathrm{B}$-module, is isomorphic to $\mathrm{B}$.
Since $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{J}$, $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ and $\mathrm{X}_0=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ have the same underlying space; if $\mathscr{J}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}$, $\mathrm{X}_0$ is the sub-prescheme defined by $\mathscr{J}$; we will denote $\mathrm{U}_0$ the prescheme induced by $\mathrm{X}_0$ on the open $\mathrm{U}$.
For every $z\in\mathfrak{J}$, set $\varphi(z)=1+z$; since $(1+z)(1\setmin z)=1$, $1+z$ is invertible in $\mathrm{A}$, and $\varphi(\mathfrak{J})$ is the kernel of the canonical surjective homomorphism of multiplicative groups $\mathrm{A}^*\to\mathrm{B}^*$; in other words, we have an exact sequence of commutative groups
\[
  0\to\mathfrak{J}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathrm{A}^*\to\mathrm{B}^*\to 1
\]
(the three last groups being multiplicative, the two first being additive).
For the same reason, for every $t\in\mathrm{A}$, we have the exact sequence
\[
  0\to\mathfrak{J}_t\xrightarrow{\varphi_t}(\mathrm{A}_t)^*\to(\mathrm{B}_{t_0})^*\to 1
\]
denoting by $t_0$ the image of $t$ in $\mathrm{B}$, since $\mathrm{B}_{t_0}=\mathrm{A}_t/\mathfrak{J}_t$; in other words, we have an exact sequence of sheaves of commutative groups on the topological space $\mathrm{X}$
\[
  0\to\mathscr{J}\xrightarrow{u}\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}^*\to\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_0}^*\to 1
\]
whence by restriction to the open $\mathrm{U}$, an exact sequence
\[
  0\to\mathscr{J}|\mathrm{U}\to\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}^*\to\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0}^*\to 1.
\]

By the cohomology exact sequence, we deduce an exact sequence
\[
\label{IV.21.13.9.1}
  \mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U},\mathscr{J})\to\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}^*)\xrightarrow{u_1}\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0}^*).
  \tag{21.13.9.1}
\]
But since $\mathfrak{J}$ is a $\mathrm{B}$-module isomorphic to $\mathrm{B}$, we have $\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U},\mathscr{J})=\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U}_0,\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0})$, and it follows from chap.~III, Part~3 (see also \cite[III, Example~III-1]{IV-41}) that
----- French corrections on page IV-4 316 -----
[id 23872]
OLD: (21.13.5)), en
NEW: (21.13.5)) en
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected):  $(X,Y)$ est parafactoriel.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}

\begin{definition}[21.13.7]
\EGAReaderTarget{ega4.c04.def.21-13-7}{IV.21.13.7}
On dit qu'un anneau local $A$ est parafactoriel si, posant
$X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ et désignant par $a$ le point fermé de $X$, le couple
$(X,\{a\})$ est parafactoriel.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.8]
\label{IV.21.13.8}
Les notations étant celles de \textup{(21.13.7)}, on pose $U=X\setmin\{a\}$. Pour que $A$ soit
parafactoriel, il faut et il suffit qu'il vérifie les conditions suivantes :
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  \item L'homomorphisme canonique
  $A=\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)\to\Gamma(U,\mathcal O_X)$ est bijectif.
  \item $\operatorname{Pic}(U)=0$.
\end{enumerate}
\oldpage[IV-4]{317}

Si en outre $A$ est noethérien, la condition \textup{(i)} équivaut à
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textup{(i \emph{bis})}]

=================== PAGE IV-4 318 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 317: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 318 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{318}
$\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U}_0,\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0})=0$ by reason of the relation $\operatorname{prof}(\mathrm{B})\geq 3$.
On the other hand, since $\mathrm{B}$ is factorial, $\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0}^*)=\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U}_0)=0$; we obtain therefore from the preceding exact sequence $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})=\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}^*)=0$.

(iv) There also exist local Noetherian rings of dimension $3$ which are integral, integrally closed and parafactorial, but not factorial.
Let indeed $\mathrm{B}$ be a local Noetherian ring, complete, integral, integrally closed, of dimension $\geq 2$ and not factorial (for example the completion of the localised ring of the integral algebra $k[\mathrm{U},\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}]/(\mathrm{W}^2\setmin\mathrm{U}\mathrm{V})$ at the ideal image of $(\mathrm{U})+(\mathrm{V})+(\mathrm{W})$).
Then it follows from what we will see below \sref{IV.21.14.2} that the local ring $\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{B}[[\mathrm{T}]]$ of formal power series in $\mathrm{T}$ over $\mathrm{B}$ is parafactorial, but it is not factorial, without which $\mathrm{B}$ would be so by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.12} below.

(v) One can show that an absolute complete-intersection local ring \sref{IV.19.3.1} of dimension $\geq 4$ is parafactorial (cf.\ \cite[XI, $3.13$~(i)]{IV-41}).

(vi) We have seen (Remark~(ii)) that every local Noetherian factorial ring $\mathrm{A}$ of dimension $2$ is parafactorial.
But there are local Noetherian rings of dimension $2$ which are parafactorial and not factorial.
One can show that, for a local Noetherian ring $\mathrm{A}$ of dimension $2$ to be parafactorial and not factorial, it is necessary and sufficient that it satisfy the following three conditions:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*\textsuperscript{o}]
  \item $\mathrm{A}$ is a Cohen-Macaulay ring (in other words $\operatorname{prof}(\mathrm{A})=2$).
  \item $\mathrm{A}$ is integral and if $\mathrm{A}'$ is its integral closure, $\mathrm{A}'$ is factorial and is a finite A-algebra.
  \item Let $\mathfrak{J}$ be the conductor of $\mathrm{A}$ in $\mathrm{A}'$ (the annihilator of the $\mathrm{A}$-module $\mathrm{A}'/\mathrm{A}$, or equivalently the largest ideal of $\mathrm{A}'$ contained in $\mathrm{A}$); set $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{J}$, $\mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{A}'/\mathfrak{J}$. Then $\dim(\mathrm{B})=1$ (which implies $\mathrm{A}'\neq\mathrm{A}$, i.e.\ $\mathrm{A}$ is not integrally closed), and the canonical map $\operatorname{Div}(\mathrm{B})\to\operatorname{Div}(\mathrm{B}')$ \sref{IV.21.4.5} is \emph{surjective}.
\end{enumerate}
One can furthermore show that these conditions imply the following property:

$4^{\mathrm{o}}$ The ring $\mathrm{B}'$ (and, a fortiori, $\mathrm{B}\subset\mathrm{B}'$) is reduced, and the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}')\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is bijective.

If we set $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$, $\mathrm{X}'=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}')$, $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$, and $\mathrm{Y}'=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}')$, so that $\mathrm{Y}$ (resp.\ $\mathrm{Y}'$) is defined by the ideal $\mathfrak{J}$ of $\mathrm{A}$ (resp.\ $\mathrm{A}'$), the structural morphism $f:\mathrm{X}'\to\mathrm{X}$ is an isomorphism of $\mathrm{X}'\setmin\mathrm{Y}'$ onto $\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg. comm.}, chap.~V, \S1, no.~5, Cor.~5 of Prop.~16). By $4^{\mathrm{o}}$, $f$ is a bijective morphism; in other words, $\mathrm{X}$ is a Noetherian local unibranch prescheme \sref{IV.6.15.1} (and in particular $\mathrm{A}'$ is a Noetherian local ring). In general, $\mathrm{X}$ is not geometrically unibranch.

The space $\mathrm{Y}$, of dimension $1$, consists of the closed point $x$ of $\mathrm{X}$ and the maximal points $y_i$ ($1\leq i\leq r$) of $\mathrm{Y}$; the unique point $y_i'$ of $\mathrm{Y}'$ above $y_i$ is likewise a maximal point of $\mathrm{Y}'$. Since $\mathrm{B}$ and $\mathrm{B}'$ are reduced, it follows that $\mathfrak{m}_z\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}',z'}=\mathfrak{m}_{z'}$ for $z=y_i$ and $z'=y_i'$; this relation is plainly also true for $z\notin\mathrm{Y}$ and the unique point $z'$ of $f^{-1}(z)$; hence it holds for every $z\in\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\{x\}$ and the unique point $z'$ of $f^{-1}(z)$. In particular, if $\mathrm{A}$ has characteristic $0$ $(\mathbf{0}$, $21.1.1)$, then $\mathrm{U}'$ is \emph{unramified} over $\mathrm{U}$ (but not \'etale in general).

We shall confine ourselves here to proving that conditions $1^{\mathrm{o}}$, $2^{\mathrm{o}}$, and $3^{\mathrm{o}}$ above are \emph{sufficient} for $\mathrm{A}$ to be parafactorial. By condition $1^{\mathrm{o}}$ and \sref{IV.21.13.8}, it suffices to show that $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})=0$. Since $\mathrm{A}'$ is factorial by $2^{\mathrm{o}}$, one has $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U}')=0$, and \sref{IV.21.8.5},~(ii) gives an exact sequence
\[
  1\to
  \left(\prod_{s\in\mathrm{S}} \sh{O}'^{*}_{\mathrm{U},s}/\sh{O}^*_{\mathrm{U},s}\right)
  \big/\operatorname{Im}\Gamma(\mathrm{U}',\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}'}^*)
  \longrightarrow\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U}')\to 0.
\]
It therefore remains to show that the second term of this sequence is reduced to the neutral element. Here we take $\mathrm{S}=\{y_i\mid 1\leq i\leq r\}$ and note that $\mathcal O'_X=f_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}'})$ is the $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Algebra $\widetilde{\mathrm{A}'}$. Let $\mathfrak{p}_i$, $\mathfrak{p}_i'$ be the prime ideals of $\mathrm{A}$ and $\mathrm{A}'$ corresponding to $y_i$, $y_i'$, and note that $\mathfrak{p}_i'\cap\mathrm{A}=\mathfrak{p}_i$. For each $i$, choose an invertible element $a_i'/s_i$ of $\mathrm{A}'_{\mathfrak{p}_i}$, with $a_i'\in\mathrm{A}'$ and $s_i\notin\mathfrak{p}_i$. Since only the quotient groups $\mathrm{A}'^*_{\mathfrak{p}_i}/\mathrm{A}^*_{\mathfrak{p}_i}$ are at issue, we may suppose that $s_i=1$ for every $i$. Let $b_i'\in\mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{A}'/\mathfrak{J}$ be the canonical image of $a_i'$, so that $b_i'/1$ is a nonzero element of $\boldsymbol{k}(y_i')=\sh{O}_{\mathrm{Y}',y_i'}$.

The open $\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{Y}$, consisting of the points $y_i$, is an affine open of the form $\mathrm{D}(t)$ in $\mathrm{Y}$, with $t\in\mathfrak{m}$, the maximal ideal of $\mathrm{A}$. There is therefore an invertible element $b'\in\mathrm{B}'_t$ whose canonical images are the $b_i'/1$. Since $t$ is regular in the integral ring $\mathrm{A}'$, we may write $b'=b''/t^m$, where $b''\in\mathrm{B}'$ is \emph{invertible}. Let $a''\in\mathrm{A}'$ represent the class $b''$; it is necessarily invertible. Then $a'=a''/t^m$ is invertible in $\mathrm{A}'_t$, and for every $i$ one has
\[
  u_i=a''\setmin a_i't^m\in\mathfrak{J},
  \qquad
  t^m a_i'=a''\setmin u_i=a''(1\setmin a''^{-1}u_i).
\]
But $a''^{-1}u_i\in\mathfrak{J}\subset\mathfrak{m}$, so $1\setmin a''^{-1}u_i$ is invertible in $\mathrm{A}$; hence the classes of $a'$ and $a_i'$ in $\mathrm{A}'^*_{\mathfrak{p}_i}/\mathrm{A}^*_{\mathfrak{p}_i}$ are the same, which completes the proof.
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 319 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{319}
For an explicit example of a parafactorial ring of dimension $2$ obtained in this way and \emph{not factorial}, consider
\[
  \mathrm{E}=\mathbb{R}[[\mathrm{U},\mathrm{V}]]/(\mathrm{U}^2+\mathrm{V}^2),
\]
whose integral closure $\mathrm{E}'$ identifies with $\mathbb{C}[[\mathrm{U}]]$ \sref{IV.6.15.11}. If $\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{E}[[\mathrm{T}]]$, its integral closure is $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{E}'[[\mathrm{T}]]$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg. comm.}, chap.~V, \S1, no.~4, Prop.~14). One verifies at once that the conductor of $\mathrm{E}$ in $\mathrm{E}'$ is the maximal ideal $\mathfrak{n}$ of $\mathrm{E}$, hence that the conductor $\mathfrak{J}$ of $\mathrm{A}$ in $\mathrm{A}'$ is $\mathfrak{n}[[\mathrm{T}]]$, and one has $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{J}\cong\mathbb{R}[[\mathrm{T}]]$, $\mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{A}'/\mathfrak{J}\cong\mathbb{C}[[\mathrm{T}]]$. Conditions $1^{\mathrm{o}}$, $2^{\mathrm{o}}$, and $3^{\mathrm{o}}$ above are therefore satisfied, although $\mathrm{A}$ is not even integrally closed.

One may vary this example: if $k$ is an algebraically closed field, the localisation at the maximal ideal generated by the images of $\mathrm{U}$, $\mathrm{V}$, and $\mathrm{W}$ of $k[\mathrm{U},\mathrm{V},\mathrm{W}]/(\mathrm{U}^2\setmin\mathrm{W}\mathrm{V}^2)$
also satisfies conditions $1^{\mathrm{o}}$, $2^{\mathrm{o}}$, and $3^{\mathrm{o}}$ above.

It may be that these three conditions even imply that each of the rings $\mathrm{B}'/\mathfrak{p}_i'$ is a discrete valuation ring, which would imply that $\mathrm{A}'$ itself is a regular ring.
\end{env}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.10]
\label{IV.21.13.10}
Let $\mathrm{X}$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $\mathrm{Y}$ a closed subset of $\mathrm{X}$.
For the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ to be parafactorial, it is necessary and sufficient that, for all $y\in\mathrm{Y}$, the local ring $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ is parafactorial.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Set $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$ and let $j:\mathrm{U}\to\mathrm{X}$ be the canonical injection.

By virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.4}, to say that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})$ is bijective is equivalent to saying that each of the local rings $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ for $y\in\mathrm{Y}$ satisfies condition (i \emph{bis}) of \sref{IV.21.13.8}.

Let us first show that if the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is parafactorial, each of the rings $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ ($y\in\mathrm{Y}$) is parafactorial.
In view of the preceding remark, everything comes down to seeing that, if we put $\mathrm{T}_y=\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y})$ and $\mathrm{U}_y=\mathrm{T}_y\setmin\{y\}$, every invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_y}$-Module $\mathscr{L}_0$ is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_y}$.
Now, when $\mathrm{V}$ ranges over the set of affine open neighbourhoods of $y$ in $\mathrm{X}$, it follows from \sref{IV.8.2.13} and \sref[I]{I.2.4.2} that the prescheme $\mathrm{U}_y$ is the projective limit of the preschemes induced by $\mathrm{X}$ on the open sets $\mathrm{V}\setmin(\mathrm{V}\cap\overline{\{y\}})$, which are quasi-compact since $\mathrm{X}$ is locally Noetherian.
Since the $\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{V}}=\mathrm{V}\setmin(\mathrm{V}\cap\overline{\{y\}})$ are separated, it then follows from \sref{IV.8.5.2},~(ii) and \sref{IV.8.5.5} that there are an affine open neighbourhood $\mathrm{V}$ of $y$ in $\mathrm{X}$ and an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{V}}}$-Module $\mathscr{L}'_{\mathrm{V}}$ such that $\mathscr{L}_0$ is the sheaf induced by $\mathscr{L}'_{\mathrm{V}}$ on $\mathrm{U}_y$.
But the hypothesis implies, by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(i) and (ii), that the pair $(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{V}\cap\overline{\{y\}})$ is parafactorial; we conclude by definition \sref{IV.21.13.1} that there exists an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{V}}$-Module $\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{V}}$ inducing $\mathscr{L}'_{\mathrm{V}}$ on $\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{V}}$. Replacing $\mathrm{V}$ if necessary by a smaller open neighbourhood of $y$, we can suppose that $\mathscr{L}_{\mathrm{V}}$ is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{V}}$, whence we conclude that $\mathscr{L}_0$ is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_y}$.

Conversely, let us prove that if all the $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ ($y\in\mathrm{Y}$) are parafactorial, the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is parafactorial.
We are evidently reduced, in view of the remark at the beginning, to showing that for every invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}$-Module $\mathscr{L}$, $j_*(\mathscr{L})$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module \sref{IV.21.13.5}.
The question being local on $\mathrm{X}$, we can suppose $\mathrm{X}$ Noetherian. The set $\mathrm{V}$ of the $x\in\mathrm{X}$ such that the restriction of $j_*(\mathscr{L})$ to an open neighbourhood of $x$ in $\mathrm{X}$ is invertible is evidently open in $\mathrm{X}$; we must show that $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{X}$. For this, suppose the contrary and put $\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{V}\neq\varnothing$. Let $y$ be a \emph{maximal} point of $\mathrm{Z}$; since $\mathrm{Z}\subset\mathrm{Y}$ by definition, $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ is by hypothesis a parafactorial ring. Replacing $\mathrm{X}$ if necessary by an open neighbourhood of $y$ in $\mathrm{X}$, we can suppose that the restriction of $j_*(\mathscr{L})$

If we then put $\mathrm{V}_1=\mathrm{V}\cup\mathrm{W}$ and denote by $\mathscr{L}_1$ the invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{V}_1}$-Module equal to $\mathscr{L}''$ in $\mathrm{W}$ and to $j_*(\mathscr{L})|\mathrm{V}$ in $\mathrm{V}$, we have $\mathrm{U}\subset\mathrm{V}_1$ and $\mathscr{L}_1|\mathrm{U}=\mathscr{L}$. We then conclude from the fact that the homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})$ is bijective and from \sref{IV.21.13.2},~\emph{b)} that $j_*(\mathscr{L})|\mathrm{V}_1$ is isomorphic to $\mathscr{L}_1$, hence invertible. But this contradicts the definition of $\mathrm{V}$, and therefore concludes the proof of \sref{IV.21.13.10}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[21.13.11]
\label{IV.21.13.11}
Let $\mathrm{X}$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $\mathrm{Y}$ a closed subset of $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$.
Suppose that the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is parafactorial and that $\mathrm{U}$ is locally factorial; in other terms \sref{IV.21.13.10}, for all $x\in\mathrm{X}$, the local ring $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is:
$1^{\mathrm{o}}$~parafactorial if $x\in\mathrm{Y}$;
$2^{\mathrm{o}}$~factorial if $x\notin\mathrm{Y}$.
Then $\mathrm{X}$ is locally factorial (in other words, $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is in fact factorial for \emph{all} $x\in\mathrm{X}$).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Suppose indeed the contrary, and let $y$ be a point of $\mathrm{Y}$ such that $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ is not factorial and has a minimal dimension among all the points of $\mathrm{Y}$ having this property.
Then, if we set $\mathrm{T}_y=\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y})$, $\mathrm{U}_y=\mathrm{T}_y\setmin\{y\}$, the hypothesis that $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ is parafactorial implies $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U}_y)=0$ and $\operatorname{prof}(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y})\geq 2$ \sref{IV.21.13.8}; moreover, the choice of $y$ implies that $\mathrm{U}_y$ is locally factorial.
But then \sref{IV.21.6.14} proves that $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ is factorial, contrary to the hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.12]
\label{IV.21.13.12}
Let $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{B}$ be two local Noetherian rings, $\rho:\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}$ a local homomorphism making $\mathrm{B}$ a flat A-module.
Then, if $\mathrm{B}$ is factorial, so is $\mathrm{A}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We already know that $\mathrm{A}$ is integral and integrally closed \sref{IV.6.5.4}; we may therefore restrict to the case $\dim(\mathrm{A})\geq 2$ and argue by induction on $n=\dim(\mathrm{A})$.
Let $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$, $a$ the closed point of $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{X}'=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$, $f:\mathrm{X}'\to\mathrm{X}$ the structural morphism, $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\{a\}$, $\mathrm{U}'=f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})$.
The local rings $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}',x'}$ at points $x'\in f^{-1}(a)$ are factorial and of dimension $\geq 2$ \sref{IV.6.1.2}, hence parafactorial \sref{IV.21.13.9},~(ii); we conclude from \sref{IV.21.13.10} that the pair $(\mathrm{X}',f^{-1}(a))$ is parafactorial.
By virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(iii), this shows that the ring $\mathrm{A}$ is parafactorial, hence $\operatorname{prof}(\mathrm{A})\geq 2$ and $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})=0$ \sref{IV.21.13.8}.
Finally, the local rings of $\mathrm{U}$ being of dimension $<n$, the induction hypothesis proves that $\mathrm{U}$ is \emph{locally factorial}; the conclusion follows then from \sref{IV.21.6.14}.
\end{proof}

\begin{env}[21.13.12.1]
\label{IV.21.13.12.1}
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 321 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{321}
We note that the statement of \sref{IV.21.13.12} where one replaces ``factorial'' by ``parafactorial'' is no longer exact, as shown by the example where $\mathrm{A}=k$ is a field and $\mathrm{B}$ the local factorial ring $k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\mathrm{T}_2]]$.
However, it follows from \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(iii) that, under the conditions of \sref{IV.21.13.12}, if $\mathfrak{m}$ denotes the maximal ideal of $\mathrm{A}$ and if $\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}$ is an \emph{ideal of definition} of $\mathrm{B}$ (i.e.\ $\dim(\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B})=0$), then, when $\mathrm{B}$ is parafactorial, so is $\mathrm{A}$.
In particular, if the completion $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$ of a local Noetherian ring is parafactorial, so is $\mathrm{A}$.
\end{env}

\begin{remark}[21.13.13]
\label{IV.21.13.13}
Some of the preceding definitions and results belong to more general considerations on the cohomology of sheaves of commutative groups, developed in chap.~III, Part~3, for which we have given an independent account here for the convenience of the reader.
Indeed, if $\mathrm{X}$ is a ringed space, there is a canonical \emph{equivalence} between the category of invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Modules and that of \emph{principal homogeneous sheaves under the sheaf of groups} $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}^*$ \sref{IV.16.5.15}.
This equivalence associates with every invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module $\mathscr{L}$ the sheaf $\mathscr{L}^*=\mathscr{I}som_{\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}}(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}},\mathscr{L})$ of germs of isomorphisms of $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$ onto $\mathscr{L}$; it is immediate that $\mathscr{L}^*$ is canonically equipped with a structure of principal homogeneous sheaf under $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}^*\cong\mathscr{I}som_{\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}}(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})$.
The verification of this equivalence of categories is immediate.
This being said, let $\mathrm{X}$ be a topological space, $\mathscr{G}$ a sheaf of groups on $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{Y}$ a closed subset of $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$, and $i$ an integer such that $0\leq i\leq 2$. We say that the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is \emph{$i$-pure} for $\mathscr{G}$ if, for every open $\mathrm{V}$ of $\mathrm{X}$, the restriction functor $\mathscr{P}\leadsto\mathscr{P}|(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V})$, from the category of principal homogeneous sheaves under the sheaf of groups $\mathscr{G}|\mathrm{V}$ into the analogous category under $\mathscr{G}|(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V})$, is \emph{faithful} ($i=0$), resp.\ \emph{fully faithful} ($i=1$), resp.\ an \emph{equivalence of categories} ($i=2$).
In the case where $\mathrm{X}$ is a ringed space and $\mathscr{G}=\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}^*$, to say that $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is $i$-pure therefore means, for $i=0$, that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})$ is \emph{injective}; for $i=1$, that this homomorphism is \emph{bijective}; and finally, for $i=2$, that the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is \emph{parafactorial} \sref{IV.21.13.3}.

Returning to the general case, recall that the morphisms of the category of principal sheaves are by definition isomorphisms.
To say that the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is $0$-pure means therefore that for every open $\mathrm{V}$ of $\mathrm{X}$, the canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{H}^0(\mathrm{V},\mathscr{G})\to\mathrm{H}^0(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V},\mathscr{G})$ is injective; to say that the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is $1$-pure means that the canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{H}^0(\mathrm{V},\mathscr{G})\to\mathrm{H}^0(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V},\mathscr{G})$ is bijective (and so also the canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{V},\mathscr{G})\to\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V},\mathscr{G})$ is injective); finally, one can show that for the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ to be $2$-pure, it is necessary and sufficient that the homomorphisms $\mathrm{H}^i(\mathrm{V},\mathscr{G})\to\mathrm{H}^i(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V},\mathscr{G})$ be bijective for $i=0$ and $i=1$.
When $\mathscr{G}$ is a sheaf of commutative groups, introduce the cohomology sheaves $\mathscr{H}^i_{\mathrm{Y}}(\mathscr{G})$ defined in chap.~III, Part~3. To say that $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is $i$-pure for $i\leq2$ then means that $\mathscr{H}^p_{\mathrm{Y}}(\mathscr{G})=0$ for $p\leq i$; in this form, the notion generalises immediately to every integer $i$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.14]
\label{IV.21.13.14}
Let $\mathrm{X}$ be a locally Noetherian normal prescheme, $(\mathrm{U}_\lambda)_{\lambda\in\mathrm{L}}$ a decreasing filtered family of opens of $\mathrm{X}$.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\emph{\alph*)}]
  \item Every $1$-codimensional cycle $\mathrm{Z}$ on $\mathrm{X}$ such that there exists an index $\lambda$ for which $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ is locally principal \sref{IV.21.6.7}, is locally principal.
  \item For all $x\in\mathrm{X}$ such that $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\geq 2$ and such that $x$ does not belong to $\bigcap_{\lambda\in\mathrm{L}}\mathrm{U}_\lambda$, the local ring $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is parafactorial.
----- French corrections on page IV-4 320 -----
[id 23739]

=================== PAGE IV-4 321 ===================

----- (English page IV-4 321: shown above in this file) -----

\oldpage[IV-4]{322}
  \item[\textup{b$'$)}] For every closed subset $\mathrm{Y}$ of $\mathrm{X}$ such that $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{X})\geq 2$ and $\mathrm{Y}\subset\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ for some $\lambda$, the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is parafactorial.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Property \emph{a)} may also be expressed as follows. If the closed set $\mathrm{N}$ of points $x\in\mathrm{X}$ at which $\mathrm{Z}$ is not principal is contained in one of the sets $\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{U}_\lambda$, then $\mathrm{Z}$ is locally principal. Since $\mathrm{X}$ is normal, the condition $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\leq 1$ implies that $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is a field or a discrete valuation ring, and therefore that $\mathrm{Z}_x$ is principal; thus necessarily $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{N},\mathrm{X})\geq 2$ \sref{IV.5.1.3}. Consequently, \emph{a)} is equivalent to:

\emph{a$'$)} If there exists a closed subset $\mathrm{Y}$ contained in one of the sets $\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{U}_\lambda$, such that $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{X})\geq 2$ and $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$ is locally principal, then $\mathrm{Z}$ is locally principal.

Condition \emph{b)} implies \emph{b$'$)} by \sref{IV.21.13.10}. Conversely, if \emph{b$'$)} holds and $x\notin\mathrm{U}_\lambda$, then $\mathrm{Y}=\overline{\{x\}}$ is contained in $\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ and $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{X})\geq 2$; it follows again from \sref{IV.21.13.10} that $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is parafactorial. This proves the equivalence of \emph{b)} and \emph{b$'$)}.

We are thus reduced to proving the equivalence of \emph{a$'$)} and \emph{b$'$)}. Since $\mathrm{X}$ is normal, for every closed subset $\mathrm{Y}$ of $\mathrm{X}$ such that $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{X})\geq 2$, one has $\operatorname{prof}_{\mathrm{Y}}(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}})\geq 2$ \sref{IV.5.8.6}. If $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$ and $j:\mathrm{U}\to\mathrm{X}$ is the canonical injection, the homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})$ is therefore bijective \sref{IV.21.13.4}. Since \emph{a$'$)} and \emph{b$'$)} are local conditions, it suffices to prove, for $\mathrm{X}$ Noetherian and normal and $\mathrm{Y}$ closed with $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{X})\geq 2$, the equivalence of:

\emph{a$''$)} For every $1$-codimensional cycle $\mathrm{Z}$ on $\mathrm{X}$, if $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}$ (where $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$) is locally principal, then $\mathrm{Z}$ is locally principal.

\emph{b$''$)} The canonical homomorphism $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{X})\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})$ is surjective.

First suppose \emph{a$''$)}, and let $\zeta_0\in\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})$. Its image in $\mathfrak{Cl}(\mathrm{U})$ \sref{IV.21.6.11} is the class of a locally principal $1$-codimensional cycle $\mathrm{Z}_0$ on $\mathrm{U}$. The hypothesis $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{X})\geq 2$ implies that the restriction homomorphism $\mathfrak{Z}^1(\mathrm{X})\to\mathfrak{Z}^1(\mathrm{U})$ is bijective; hence $\mathrm{Z}_0=\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}$ for a $1$-codimensional cycle $\mathrm{Z}$ on $\mathrm{X}$. By \emph{a$''$)}, $\mathrm{Z}$ is locally principal. It follows from \sref{IV.21.6.11} that the image of $\mathrm{Z}$ in $\mathfrak{Cl}(\mathrm{X})$ is the image of a unique element $\zeta\in\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{X})$, and it is then clear that $\zeta_0$ is the image of $\zeta$.

Conversely, suppose \emph{b$''$)}, and let $\mathrm{Z}$ be a $1$-codimensional cycle on $\mathrm{X}$ such that $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}$ is locally principal. The image of $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}$ in $\mathfrak{Cl}(\mathrm{U})$ is the image of a unique element $\zeta_0\in\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})$ \sref{IV.21.6.11}. By hypothesis, there exists $\zeta\in\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{X})$ whose image in $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})$ is $\zeta_0$; the image of $\zeta$ in $\mathfrak{Cl}(\mathrm{X})$ is therefore the class of a locally principal $1$-codimensional cycle $\mathrm{Z}'$ on $\mathrm{X}$ such that $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}$ and $\mathrm{Z}'|\mathrm{U}$ are linearly equivalent. But $\mathrm{U}$ is schematically dense in $\mathrm{X}$, and the isomorphism $\mathfrak{Z}^1(\mathrm{X})\to\mathfrak{Z}^1(\mathrm{U})$ carries $\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathrm{princ}}^1(\mathrm{X})$ onto $\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathrm{princ}}^1(\mathrm{U})$; hence $\mathrm{Z}$ and $\mathrm{Z}'$ are linearly equivalent. Since $\mathrm{Z}'$ is locally principal, so is $\mathrm{Z}$.
\end{proof}

  \item For all $x\in\mathrm{X}$ which is not a generisation of a point of $\mathrm{S}$ (in other words, such that $\overline{\{x\}}\cap\mathrm{S}=\varnothing$) and such that $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\geq 2$, the local ring $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is parafactorial.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{proof}
It is clear that the set $\mathrm{S}'$ of generisations in $\mathrm{X}$ of points of $\mathrm{S}$ is the intersection of the open neighbourhoods of $\mathrm{S}$. We may restrict to the case where $\mathrm{X}$ is Noetherian, since properties \emph{a)} and \emph{b)} are local on $\mathrm{X}$. Every $1$-codimensional cycle on $\mathrm{X}$ which is principal at the points of $\mathrm{S}$ is also principal at the points of an open neighbourhood of $\mathrm{S}$; condition \emph{a)} of \sref{IV.21.13.15} is therefore condition \emph{a)} of \sref{IV.21.13.14} applied to the family $(\mathrm{U}_\lambda)$ of open neighbourhoods of $\mathrm{S}$. The corollary now follows from the equivalence of \emph{a)} and \emph{b)} in \sref{IV.21.13.14}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[21.13.16]
\label{IV.21.13.16}
Under the general conditions of \sref{IV.21.13.14}, suppose moreover $\varinjlim\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U}_\lambda)=0$.
Then condition \emph{a)} of \sref{IV.21.13.14} is also equivalent to the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[c)] \emph{Every $1$-codimensional cycle on $\mathrm{X}$ whose support is contained in one of the sets $\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ is locally principal.}
\end{enumerate}

We can restrict to the case where $\mathrm{X}$ is irreducible and all the $\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ are non-empty.
It is clear that \emph{a)} implies \emph{c)}, since if the $1$-codimensional cycle $\mathrm{Z}$ on $\mathrm{X}$ has its support in $\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{U}_\lambda$, $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}_\lambda=0$, hence $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ is locally principal.
Conversely, \emph{c)} implies \emph{a)}: indeed, let $\mathrm{Z}$ be a $1$-codimensional cycle on $\mathrm{X}$ such that $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ is locally principal; since $\mathrm{X}$ is normal, $\mathrm{Z}|\mathrm{U}_\lambda=\operatorname{cyc}(\mathrm{D}_\lambda)$, where $\mathrm{D}_\lambda$ is a divisor on $\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ \sref{IV.21.6.9},{}~(i), and the hypothesis implies (by virtue of \sref{IV.21.3.4}) that there is a set $\mathrm{U}_\mu\subset\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ such that $\mathrm{D}_\mu=\mathrm{D}_\lambda|\mathrm{U}_\mu$ is equivalent to $0$.
If $\mathrm{D}_\mu=\operatorname{div}(f_\mu)$, where $f_\mu$ is a regular rational function on $\mathrm{U}_\mu$, $f_\mu$ can be considered as a regular rational function on $\mathrm{X}$. If $\mathrm{Z}'=\operatorname{cyc}(\operatorname{div}(f_\mu))$, we therefore see that $\mathrm{Z}''=\mathrm{Z}\setmin\mathrm{Z}'$ has its support in $\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{U}_\mu$; by virtue of the hypothesis, $\mathrm{Z}''$ is locally principal, whence the conclusion.

The condition $\varinjlim\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U}_\lambda)=0$ will be satisfied in particular if $(\mathrm{U}_\lambda)$ is the family of open neighbourhoods of \emph{a point} $z\in\mathrm{X}$, since for every invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_\lambda}$-Module $\mathscr{L}_\lambda$, there exists by definition a $\mathrm{U}_\mu\subset\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ such that $\mathscr{L}_\lambda|\mathrm{U}_\mu$ is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_\mu}$.
We see therefore that, in the statement of \sref{IV.21.13.15}, if $\mathrm{S}=\{z\}$, conditions \emph{a)} and \emph{b)} are equivalent also to the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[c)] \emph{Every $1$-codimensional cycle on $\mathrm{X}$ whose support does not contain $z$ is locally principal.}
\end{enumerate}

If moreover $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{X})=0$, we shall conclude that this condition implies that every $1$-codimensional cycle whose support does not contain $z$ is \emph{principal}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Ramanujam-Samuel theorem}
\label{subsection:IV.21.14}
\label{IV.21.14}

\begin{theorem}[21.14.1]
\label{IV.21.14.1}
\textup{(Ramanujam-Samuel)} ---
Let $\mathrm{A}$ be a local Noetherian ring with maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}$, such that its completion $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$ is integral and integrally closed.
Let $\mathrm{B}$ be a local Noetherian ring such that $\dim(\mathrm{B})>\dim(\mathrm{A})$, and let $\rho:\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{B}$ be a local homomorphism making $\mathrm{B}$ a formally smooth $\mathrm{A}$-algebra (for the preadic topologies $(\mathbf{0}$, $19.3.1)$) and such that the residue field of $\mathrm{B}$ is finite over that of $\mathrm{A}$.
Then every $1$-codimensional cycle on $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ which is principal at the point $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}$, is a principal $1$-codimensional cycle.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

=================== PAGE IV-4 323 ===================

If $k=\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{m}$ is the residue field of $\mathrm{A}$, then $\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}$ is a formally smooth $k$-algebra for its preadic topology $(\mathbf{0}$, $19.3.5)$, hence regular and in particular integral. Thus $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}$ is indeed a prime ideal of $\mathrm{B}$, which justifies the statement.
It evidently all amounts to proving that every prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}$ of height one of $\mathrm{B}$ not contained in $\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}$ is \emph{principal}.

Let $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$, $\widehat{\mathrm{B}}$ be the completions of $\mathrm{A}$ and $\mathrm{B}$ respectively, so that the maximal ideal of $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$ is $\mathfrak{m}\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$; we know $(\mathbf{0}$, $19.3.6)$ that $\widehat{\mathrm{B}}$ is a formally smooth $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$-algebra for the adic topologies. Let $k'$ be the residue field of $\widehat{\mathrm{B}}$, a finite extension of $k$; there exists a local homomorphism $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}\to\mathrm{C}$, where $\mathrm{C}$ is a Noetherian local ring which is a \emph{finite} and \emph{flat} (hence free) $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$-module and is such that $\mathrm{C}/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{C}$ is isomorphic to $k'$ $(\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{III}}$, $10.3.1)$; we deduce that $\mathrm{C}$ is \emph{complete}, and it then follows from \sref{IV.7.5.1}, \sref{IV.7.5.3} and \sref{IV.6.5.4},~(ii) that $\mathrm{C}$ is \emph{integral and integrally closed}. Moreover, $\mathrm{D}=\widehat{\mathrm{B}}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{C}$ is a complete semi-local ring, a direct product of complete local rings one of which, $\mathrm{D}_0$, has residue field $k'$ (since $k'\otimes_k k'$ is a direct product of local rings one of which is isomorphic to $k'$). Since $\mathrm{D}$ is formally smooth over $\mathrm{C}$, so is $\mathrm{D}_0$; consequently $\mathrm{D}_0/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{D}_0$ is a formally smooth $k'$-algebra with residue field $k'$, which implies that it is $k'$-isomorphic to an algebra of formal power series $k'[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$ $(\mathbf{0}$, $19.6.4)$; we conclude, by $(\mathbf{0}$, $19.7.1.5)$, that $\mathrm{D}_0$ is $\mathrm{C}$-isomorphic to $\mathrm{C}[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$, and consequently \emph{integral and integrally closed} (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~V, \S1, no.~4, Prop.~14). Since the morphisms $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{D}_0)\to\operatorname{Spec}(\widehat{\mathrm{B}})$ and $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{D}_0)\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ are faithfully flat, we deduce that $\mathrm{B}$ and $\widehat{\mathrm{B}}$ are also \emph{integral and integrally closed} \sref{IV.2.1.13}. This proves that the ideal $\mathfrak{q}\mathrm{D}_0$ is divisorial (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \S1, no.~10, Prop.~15) and not contained in $\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{D}_0$, otherwise we would have $\mathfrak{q}=(\mathfrak{q}\mathrm{D}_0)\cap\mathrm{B}\subset(\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{D}_0)\cap\mathrm{B}=\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}=\mathfrak{p}$ by faithful flatness $(\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{I}}$, $6.5.1)$. Hence none of the height-one prime ideals $\mathfrak{r}_h$ of $\mathrm{D}_0$ containing $\mathfrak{q}\mathrm{D}_0$ is contained in $\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{D}_0$. If these ideals are principal, then $\mathfrak{q}\mathrm{D}_0$ is principal, since the divisors (in Bourbaki's sense) of $\mathfrak{q}\mathrm{D}_0$ and of a product of powers of the $\mathfrak{r}_h$ are equal (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \S1, no.~4, Prop.~5). As $\mathfrak{q}\mathrm{D}_0\cap\mathrm{B}=\mathfrak{q}$, faithful flatness then shows that $\mathfrak{q}$ is principal \sref{IV.2.5.2}, using the fact that $\mathrm{B}$ is local.

We are thus reduced to proving the theorem in the particular case where $\mathrm{A}$ is complete and $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$. Let us first show that we may reduce to $n=1$. We argue by induction on $n$, and choose $f\in\mathfrak{q}$ with $f\notin\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}$. It is enough to show that there exists an $\mathrm{A}$-automorphism $\sigma$ of $\mathrm{B}$ such that $\sigma(f)$ does not belong to
\[
  \mathfrak{p}'=\mathfrak{p}+\mathrm{B}\mathrm{T}_1+\ldots+\mathrm{B}\mathrm{T}_{n-1}.
\]
Indeed, if $\mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{A}[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_{n-1}]]$, then $\mathrm{B}'$ is complete and has maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}'+\mathrm{B}'\mathrm{T}_1+\ldots+\mathrm{B}'\mathrm{T}_{n-1}=\mathfrak{m}'$. Since $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{B}'[[\mathrm{T}_n]]$ and $\mathfrak{p}'=\mathfrak{m}'\mathrm{B}$, the induction hypothesis, taking into account that $\mathrm{B}'$ is integral and integrally closed, shows that $\sigma(\mathfrak{q})$ is principal in $\mathrm{B}$, and hence so is $\mathfrak{q}$. Now, if $\overline{f}\in k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$ is the image of $f$, then $\overline{f}\neq0$; one knows (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \S3, no.~7, Lemma~3) that there is an $\mathrm{A}$-automorphism $\sigma$ of $\mathrm{B}$ such that $\overline{(\sigma(f))}(0,\ldots,0,\mathrm{T}_n)\neq0$, which plainly implies $\sigma(f)\notin\mathfrak{p}'$.

Suppose therefore $n=1$, and write $\mathrm{T}$ instead of $\mathrm{T}_1$, so that $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}[[\mathrm{T}]]$. It is enough to show that the ideal $\mathfrak{q}\cap\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$ of the polynomial ring $\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$ is principal.

Soit $A$ un anneau local noethérien d'idéal

$(\mathbf 0, 19.3.1)$\textup{)} et tel que le corps résiduel de $B$ soit fini sur celui de $A$.
Alors tout cycle $1$-codimensionnel sur $\operatorname{Spec}(B)$ qui est principal au
point $\mathfrak p=\mathfrak mB$, est un cycle $1$-codimensionnel principal.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Si 

=================== PAGE IV-4 325 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 324: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 325 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{325}
Choose $f_0\in\mathfrak{q}$ with $f_0\notin\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}$; it is a formal power series whose reduced series $\overline{f}_0$ is nonzero. By the preparation theorem (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \S3, no.~8, Prop.~5), for every $f\in\mathfrak{q}$ there exist $g\in\mathrm{B}$ and $r\in\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$ such that $f=gf_0+r$, so $r\in\mathfrak{q}\cap\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$. On the other hand (\emph{loc.\ cit.}, Prop.~6), there exist a nonconstant distinguished polynomial $\mathrm{F}_0\in\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$ and a unit $u\in\mathrm{B}$ such that $f_0=u\mathrm{F}_0$; hence $\mathrm{F}_0\in\mathfrak{q}\cap\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$. Thus, putting $\mathfrak{q}_1=\mathfrak{q}\cap\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$, one has $\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}_1\mathrm{B}$. Since $\mathrm{B}$ is flat over $\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$ $(\mathbf{0}_{\mathrm{I}}$, $7.3.3)$, it follows from Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \S1, no.~10, Prop.~15 that $\mathfrak{q}_1$ is a height-one prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$.

Necessarily $\mathfrak{q}_1\cap\mathrm{A}=0$. Otherwise $\mathfrak{q}_1\cap\mathrm{A}$ would have height at least $1$, and \sref{IV.5.5.3} would give $\mathfrak{q}_1=(\mathfrak{q}_1\cap\mathrm{A})\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$; since $\mathfrak{q}_1\cap\mathrm{A}\subset\mathfrak{m}$, this would imply $\mathfrak{q}_1\subset\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$, contrary to the hypothesis on $\mathfrak{q}$.

Let $\mathrm{K}$ be the field of fractions of $\mathrm{A}$. Then $\mathfrak{q}_1\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]$ is a prime ideal distinct from both $0$ and $\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]$, hence is of the form $h\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]$, where
\[
  h(\mathrm{T})=\mathrm{T}^m+a_1\mathrm{T}^{m-1}+\ldots+a_m,
  \qquad m\geq1,
\]
the $a_i$ belonging to $\mathrm{K}$ and $h$ being irreducible in $\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]$. We saw above that $\mathfrak{q}_1$ contains a nonconstant distinguished polynomial $\mathrm{F}_0\in\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$. If $t$ is the class of $\mathrm{T}$ in $\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]/\mathfrak{q}_1\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]$, then $t$ is a root of $\mathrm{F}_0$ in an extension of $\mathrm{K}$, and therefore $h$ divides $\mathrm{F}_0$ in $\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]$. Since $h$ and $\mathrm{F}_0$ are monic, the coefficients $a_i$ of $h$ are integral over $\mathrm{A}$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~V, \S1, no.~3, Prop.~11), and thus belong to $\mathrm{A}$ because $\mathrm{A}$ is integrally closed. In other words,
\[
  h\in\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]\cap\mathfrak{q}_1\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]=\mathfrak{q}_1
\]
(Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~II, \S1, no.~5, Prop.~11). Since every polynomial $g\in\mathfrak{q}_1$ is divisible by $h$ in $\mathrm{K}[\mathrm{T}]$ and $h$ is monic, the coefficients of $g/h$ belong to $\mathrm{A}$; hence $\mathfrak{q}_1=h\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}]$. This completes the proof.
\end{proof}

The statement \sref{IV.21.14.1} is \emph{equivalent} to the following:

\begin{corollary}[21.14.2]
\label{IV.21.14.2}
Under the hypotheses of \sref{IV.21.14.1} on $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{B}$ and $\rho$, for every prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}$ of $\mathrm{B}$ not contained in $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{B}$ and such that $\dim(\mathrm{B}_{\mathfrak{q}})\geq 2$, the ring $\mathrm{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}$ is parafactorial.
In particular, if $\dim(\mathrm{B}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}k)>0$ \textup{(}i.e.\ $\dim\mathrm{B}>\dim\mathrm{A}$ $((\mathbf{0}$, $19.7.1)$ and $(6.1.1)))$, the ring $\mathrm{B}$ is parafactorial.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, we saw in the proof of \sref{IV.21.14.1} that the conditions of the statement imply that $\mathrm{B}$ is integral and integrally closed; and the equivalence of the statements \sref{IV.21.14.1} and \sref{IV.21.14.2} follows then from \sref{IV.21.13.15} applied to $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ and $\mathrm{S}=\{\mathfrak{p}\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[21.14.3]
\label{IV.21.14.3}
Let $\mathrm{S}$ be a normal prescheme, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{S}$ a smooth morphism.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  \item If $\mathrm{S}$ is locally Noetherian (hence also $\mathrm{X}$), then, for all $x\in\mathrm{X}$ such that $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\geq 2$ and such that $x$ is not a maximal point of its fibre $f^{-1}(f(x))$, the ring $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is parafactorial.
  
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 326 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{326}
  Every $1$-codimensional cycle $\mathrm{Z}$ on $\mathrm{X}$ such that $\operatorname{Supp}(\mathrm{Z})$ contains no irreducible component of any fibre $\mathrm{X}_s$, $f^{-1}(s)=\mathrm{X}_s$, is locally principal.
  \item Let $\mathrm{Y}$ be a closed subset of $\mathrm{X}$ containing no irreducible component of any fibre $\mathrm{X}_s$, and such that, for every maximal point $\eta$ of $\mathrm{S}$, $\mathrm{Y}_\eta=\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}_\eta$ has codimension $\geq 2$ in $\mathrm{X}_\eta$.
  Then the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is parafactorial.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We note that the conditions of (ii) are satisfied if $\mathrm{Y}$ is a closed subset of $\mathrm{X}$ such that, for all $s\in\mathrm{S}$, $\mathrm{Y}_s=\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}_s$ is of codimension $\geq 2$ in $\mathrm{X}_s$.

To prove (i), suppose first that $\mathrm{Y}=\overline{\{x\}}$. There is an open neighbourhood $\mathrm{V}$ of $x$ in $\mathrm{X}$ such that $\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{V}$ satisfy the conditions of (ii). Indeed, the hypothesis and \sref{IV.9.5.3} allow us to choose $\mathrm{V}$ so that $\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{V}$ contains no irreducible component of a fibre $\mathrm{X}_s$. Moreover, for every maximal point $\eta$ of $\mathrm{S}$ for which $\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}_\eta\neq\varnothing$, the points $z$ of $\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}_\eta$ are specializations of $x$; hence $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},z})\geq2$. Since $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},z}=\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_\eta,z}$ (the prescheme $\mathrm{S}$ being reduced at $\eta$, by \sref{IV.2.1.13}), $\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}_\eta$ has codimension $\geq2$ in $\mathrm{X}_\eta$. Thus (ii) makes the pair $(\mathrm{V},\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{V})$ parafactorial, and \sref{IV.21.13.10} shows that $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is parafactorial. This proves the first assertion of (i).

For the second assertion, it is enough to consider the case $\mathrm{Z}=\overline{\{z\}}$, where $z\in\mathrm{X}^{(1)}$. Since $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},z}$ is an integrally closed Noetherian local ring of dimension $1$, it is a discrete valuation ring, and $\mathrm{Z}$ is therefore principal at $z$. At every other point $x\in\operatorname{Supp}(\mathrm{Z})$, one has $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\geq2$, and by hypothesis $x$ is not a maximal point of its fibre; hence $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is parafactorial. Apply \sref{IV.21.13.15}, taking for $\mathrm{S}$ the set formed by $z$ and by the maximal points of the fibres of $f$. The cycle $\mathrm{Z}$ is principal at the points of this set, since $z$ is the only point of it belonging to $\operatorname{Supp}(\mathrm{Z})$; and condition \emph{b)} of \sref{IV.21.13.15} is plainly satisfied. It follows that $\mathrm{Z}$ is locally principal.

It remains to prove (ii). Put $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$, and let $j:\mathrm{U}\to\mathrm{X}$ be the canonical injection. Since the hypotheses and the conclusion are local on $\mathrm{S}$ and on $\mathrm{X}$ by \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(i), we may assume that $\mathrm{S}$ and $\mathrm{X}$ are affine; then $f$ is of finite presentation. Since the fibres of $f$ are regular, the hypothesis on $\mathrm{Y}$ implies that, for every $x\in\mathrm{Y}$,
\[
  \operatorname{prof}(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_{f(x)},x})
  =\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_{f(x)},x})\geq1.
\]
It follows from \sref{IV.19.9.8} that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})$ is injective. Moreover, replacing $\mathrm{Y}$ by a larger closed subset by the method used in the proof of \sref{IV.19.9.8}, and using \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(ii), we may suppose that $\mathrm{Y}$ is defined by an ideal of finite type in the ring of $\mathrm{X}$. Consequently $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$ is quasi-compact and quasi-separated, and $\mathrm{Y}$ is constructible.

For every invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}$-Module $\mathscr{L}$ and every $x\in\mathrm{Y}$, it is enough to establish the existence of an open neighbourhood $\mathrm{V}$ of $x$ in $\mathrm{X}$ such that: $1^{\mathrm{o}}$ the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{V}}\to(j_{\mathrm{V}})_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V}})$ is surjective; and $2^{\mathrm{o}}$ there is an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{V}}$-Module $\mathscr{L}'_{\mathrm{V}}$ whose restriction to $\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V}$ is isomorphic to $\mathscr{L}|(\mathrm{U}\cap\mathrm{V})$ (\sref{IV.21.13.5} and \sref{IV.21.13.3}).

Set $s=f(x)$. We first reduce to the case $\mathrm{S}=\mathrm{S}_0=\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S},s})$. Let $(\mathrm{S}_\nu)$ be a fundamental system of affine open neighbourhoods of $s$ in $\mathrm{S}$, and put $\mathrm{X}_\nu=f^{-1}(\mathrm{S}_\nu)$, $\mathrm{Y}_\nu=\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}_\nu$, $\mathrm{U}_\nu=\mathrm{X}_\nu\setmin\mathrm{Y}_\nu$, with canonical injection $j_\nu:\mathrm{U}_\nu\to\mathrm{X}_\nu$. Then $\mathrm{X}_0=\mathrm{X}\times_{\mathrm{S}}\mathrm{S}_0$ is the projective limit of the $\mathrm{X}_\nu$ (8.1.2, \emph{a)}). Put $\mathrm{Y}_0=\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}_0$, $\mathrm{U}_0=\mathrm{X}_0\setmin\mathrm{Y}_0$, and let $j_0:\mathrm{U}_0\to\mathrm{X}_0$ be the canonical injection. If $p_\nu:\mathrm{X}_0\to\mathrm{X}_\nu$ denotes the affine projection, then
\[
  (j_0)_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0})
  =p_\nu^*((j_\nu)_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_\nu}))
  \qquad (\mathbf{II},\ 1.5.2),
\]
and the canonical homomorphism $\rho_0:\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_0}\to(j_0)_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0})$ is $p_\nu^*(\rho_\nu)$. Since $\rho_0$ is surjective by hypothesis, $\rho_\nu$ is surjective for sufficiently large $\nu$ \sref{IV.8.5.7}.

Let $\mathscr{L}_0$ be the restriction of $\mathscr{L}$ to $\mathrm{U}_0$. The $\mathrm{X}_\nu$ are affine, hence quasi-compact and quasi-separated. By hypothesis there exists an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_0}$-Module $\mathscr{L}'_0$ extending $\mathscr{L}_0$. For sufficiently large $\nu$ there is a quasi-coherent $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_\nu}$-Module $\mathscr{L}'_\nu$ of finite presentation such that $\mathscr{L}'_0\simeq p_\nu^*(\mathscr{L}'_\nu)$ \sref{IV.8.5.2}; by \sref{IV.8.5.5} we may suppose that $\mathscr{L}'_\nu$ is invertible. Finally, since the $\mathrm{U}_\nu$ are quasi-compact and quasi-separated and $\mathscr{L}'_0|\mathrm{U}_0\simeq\mathscr{L}_0$, for sufficiently large $\nu$ the modules $\mathscr{L}'_\nu|\mathrm{U}_\nu$ and $\mathscr{L}_\nu=\mathscr{L}|\mathrm{U}_\nu$ are isomorphic \sref{IV.8.5.2.5}. This proves the reduction.

\begin{proposition}[21.14.3]
\label{IV.21.14.3}
Soient $S$ un préschéma normal, $f:X\to S$ un morphisme lisse.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  \item Si $S$ est localement noethérien \textup{(}donc aussi $X$\textup{)}, alors, pour tout $x\in X$
  tel que $\dim(\mathcal O_{X,x})\geq2$ et que $x$ ne soit pas point maximal de sa
  fibre $f^{-1}(f(x))$, l'anneau $\mathcal O_{X,x}$ est parafactoriel. Tout cycle
  $1$-codimensionnel $Z$ sur $X$ tel que $\operatorname{Supp}(Z)$ n

[id 23940]

NEW: et telle que pour tout point maximal
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): ionnel $Z$ sur $X$ tel que $\operatorname{Supp}(Z)$ ne contienne aucune
  composante irréductible d'une fibre $f^{-1}(s)=X_s$, est localement principal.
  \item Soit $Y$ une partie fermée de $X$ ne contenant aucune composante irréductible
  d'une fibre $X_s$, et telle que pour tout point maximal $\eta$ de $S$,
  $Y_\eta=Y\cap X_\eta$ soit de codimension $\geq2$ dans $X_\eta$. Alors le couple
  $(X,Y)$ est parafactoriel.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
On notera que les conditions de (ii) sont remplies si $Y$ est une partie fermée de

  $(X,Y)$ est parafactoriel.

\begin{proof}
On notera que les conditions de (ii) sont remplies si $Y$ est une partie fermée de $X$
telle que, pour \emph{tout} $s\in S$, $Y_s=Y\cap X_s$ soit de codimension $\geq2$ dans
$X_s$.

Pour prouver (21.14.3), notons d'abord que sous les hypothèses de (i), si l'on
\oldpage[IV-4]{326}
pose $Y=\overline{\{x\}}$, il existe un voisinage ouvert $V$ de $x$ dans $X$ tel que
$Y\cap V$ et $V$ vérifient les con

=================== PAGE IV-4 327 ===================
----- (English page IV-4 326: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 327 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{327}
We are thus reduced to the case $\mathrm{S}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$, $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$, where $\mathrm{A}$ is local. Since $\mathrm{X}$ is normal and $f$ smooth, $\mathrm{S}$ is normal \sref{IV.17.5.7}; hence $\mathrm{A}$ is integral and integrally closed. Regard $\mathrm{A}$ as the filtered direct limit of its $\bb{Z}$-subalgebras of finite type. The integral closure of such a subalgebra is a subring of $\mathrm{A}$ and is again a $\bb{Z}$-algebra of finite type $(\mathbf{0},\ 7.8.3,\ \text{(ii), (iii), and (vi)})$. Thus $\mathrm{A}$ is the filtered direct limit of integrally closed $\bb{Z}$-subalgebras $\mathrm{A}_\lambda$ of finite type. By \sref{IV.1.8.4.2}, for some $\lambda$ there is an $\mathrm{A}_\lambda$-algebra $\mathrm{B}_\lambda$ of finite type such that, up to an $\mathrm{A}$-isomorphism,
\[
  \mathrm{B}=\mathrm{B}_\lambda\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_\lambda}\mathrm{A}.
\]
Put $\mathrm{S}_\lambda=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}_\lambda)$ and $\mathrm{X}_\lambda=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}_\lambda)$. If $p_\lambda:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{X}_\lambda$ is the canonical projection, we may also suppose, since $\mathrm{Y}$ is constructible, that $\mathrm{Y}=p_\lambda^{-1}(\mathrm{Y}_\lambda)$ for a closed subset $\mathrm{Y}_\lambda$ of $\mathrm{X}_\lambda$ \sref{IV.8.3.11}. Write $f_\lambda:\mathrm{X}_\lambda\to\mathrm{S}_\lambda$ for the structural morphism. By transitivity of fibres and \sref{IV.4.2.6}, after increasing $\lambda$ we may suppose that $\mathrm{Y}_\lambda$ contains no irreducible component of any fibre of $f_\lambda$. Since $f$ is smooth, we may likewise suppose that $f_\lambda$ is smooth \sref{IV.17.7.8}. Finally, the image $\eta_\lambda$ in $\mathrm{S}_\lambda$ of the generic point $\eta$ of $\mathrm{S}$ is the generic point of $\mathrm{S}_\lambda$, and
\[
  \operatorname{codim}((\mathrm{Y}_\lambda)_{\eta_\lambda},
  (\mathrm{X}_\lambda)_{\eta_\lambda})
  =\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y}_\eta,\mathrm{X}_\eta)\geq2
\]
by \sref{IV.6.1.4}. We therefore see that $\mathrm{S}_\lambda$, $\mathrm{X}_\lambda$ and $\mathrm{Y}_\lambda$ satisfy all the hypotheses of (ii), and, putting $\mathrm{X}_\mu=\mathrm{X}_\lambda\times_{\mathrm{S}_\lambda}\mathrm{S}_\mu$ for $\lambda\leq\mu$, the same holds for $\mathrm{S}_\mu$, $\mathrm{X}_\mu$ and $\mathrm{Y}_\mu$. Let us show that if we prove that the pair $(\mathrm{X}_\mu,\mathrm{Y}_\mu)$ is parafactorial for every $\mu\geq\lambda$, the same holds for $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$. Indeed, let $\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}\setmin\mathrm{Y}$, $\mathrm{U}_\lambda=\mathrm{X}_\lambda\setmin\mathrm{Y}_\lambda$, and let $j:\mathrm{U}\to\mathrm{X}$, $j_\lambda:\mathrm{U}_\lambda\to\mathrm{X}_\lambda$ be the canonical injections; the projection $p_\lambda:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{X}_\lambda$ being an affine morphism, we have $j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})=p_\lambda^*((j_\lambda)_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_\lambda}))$ $(\mathbf{II}$, $1.5.2)$, and consequently the canonical homomorphism $\rho:\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\to j_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})$ is none other than $p_\lambda^*(\rho_\lambda)$, where $\rho_\lambda:\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_\lambda}\to(j_\lambda)_*(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_\lambda})$ is the canonical homomorphism; since the latter is bijective by hypothesis, so is $\rho$. On the other hand, for every invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}}$-Module $\mathscr{L}$, there exist a $\mu\geq\lambda$ and an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_\mu}$-Module $\mathscr{L}_\mu$ such that $\mathscr{L}=p_\mu'^*(\mathscr{L}_\mu)$ (denoting by $p_\mu':\mathrm{U}\to\mathrm{U}_\mu$ the restriction of $p_\mu$) (\hyperref[IV.8.5.2]{8.5.2} and \hyperref[IV.8.5.5]{8.5.5}), $\mathrm{U}_\lambda$ being quasi-compact since $\mathrm{X}_\lambda$ is Noetherian. By hypothesis, there exists an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_\mu}$-Module $\mathscr{L}'_\mu$ such that $\mathscr{L}'_\mu|\mathrm{U}_\mu$ is isomorphic to $\mathscr{L}_\mu$; $\mathscr{L}'=p_\mu^*(\mathscr{L}'_\mu)$ is then an invertible $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$-Module such that $\mathscr{L}'|\mathrm{U}$ is isomorphic to $\mathscr{L}$, which proves our assertion.

We are thus reduced to proving (ii) when the ring $\mathrm{A}$ is an integrally closed $\bb{Z}$-algebra of finite type; since the local rings $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S},s}$ of $\mathrm{S}$ are then integrally closed excellent rings, their \emph{completions} are also integrally closed \sref{IV.7.8.3},~(ii), (iii) and (vii). By \sref{IV.21.13.10}, it is enough to show that $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$ is parafactorial for every $x\in\mathrm{Y}$. Choose a closed point $y$ of $\mathrm{Y}_{f(x)}$ which is a specialization of $x$ \sref{IV.5.1.11}, and put $s=f(x)=f(y)$. The completion of $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S},s}$ is integrally closed, and $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ is a formally smooth $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S},s}$-algebra for the preadic topologies \sref{IV.17.5.3}. Since $\mathrm{Y}_s$ contains no irreducible component of $\mathrm{X}_s$,
\[
  \dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})>\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S},s})
  \qquad\text{by \sref{IV.17.5.8}}.
\]
If $s\neq\eta$, then $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S},s})\geq1$; if $s=\eta$, the hypothesis gives $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\geq2$. In either case $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\geq2$. Furthermore, the prime ideal of $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$ corresponding to $x$ is not contained in $\mathfrak{m}_s\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$, because $\mathrm{Y}$ contains no irreducible component of $\mathrm{X}_s$. Finally $y$ is closed in $\mathrm{X}_s$, so $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$ is a finite extension of $\boldsymbol{k}(s)$ $(\mathbf{I},\ 6.4.2)$. We may therefore apply \sref{IV.21.14.2} with $\mathrm{A}=\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S},s}$, $\mathrm{B}=\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},y}$, and $\mathrm{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}=\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$. This completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{env}[21.14.4]
\label{IV.21.14.4}
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 328 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{328}
\emph{Remarks}. ---
(i) It is possible that the statements \sref{IV.21.14.1} and \sref{IV.21.14.2} remain valid \emph{without hypothesis} on the residue field of $\mathrm{B}$.
The statement with this generality would be equivalent, by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.15} and \sref{IV.21.13.12.1}, to the following: let $\mathrm{A}$ be a complete, integral, integrally closed Noetherian local ring, and let $\mathrm{B}$ be a Noetherian local ring which is a formally smooth $\mathrm{A}$-algebra, such that $\dim(\mathrm{B})>\dim(\mathrm{A})$; then $\mathrm{B}$ is parafactorial.

(ii) We will see in chap.~VI, using a technique of ``finite descent'' applied to the morphism $\mathrm{Y}'\to\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$, where $\mathrm{Y}'$ is the normalization of $\mathrm{Y}$, that the conclusion of \sref{IV.21.14.1} (or of \sref{IV.21.14.2}) remains valid when the hypothesis that $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$ is integrally closed is replaced by the hypothesis that $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$ is reduced, provided that $\dim\mathrm{B}\geq\dim\mathrm{A}+2$.
If this last condition is replaced by $\dim\mathrm{B}\geq\dim\mathrm{A}+3$, the hypothesis that $\widehat{\mathrm{A}}$ is reduced may even be omitted.
Similarly, the conclusion of \sref{IV.21.14.3} remains valid by replacing the hypothesis that $\mathrm{X}$ is normal by the hypothesis that $\mathrm{X}$ is reduced, provided that $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\geq\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S},f(x)})+2$.

(iii) In chap.~III, Part~3, we prove that, if $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{S}$ is a smooth morphism and $\mathrm{Y}$ is a closed locally constructible subset of $\mathrm{X}$ such that, for every $s\in\mathrm{S}$, $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y}_s,\mathrm{X}_s)\geq3$ (with the notation of \sref{IV.21.14.3}), then the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is parafactorial.
This conclusion is no longer valid when we only suppose $\operatorname{codim}(\mathrm{Y}_s,\mathrm{X}_s)\geq 2$ for all $s\in\mathrm{S}$ and when we do not suppose $\mathrm{X}$ reduced.
For example, let $k$ be a field, let $\mathrm{A}=k[\mathrm{T}]/(\mathrm{T}^2)$ be the algebra of dual numbers over $k$, and put $\mathrm{S}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ and $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}_1,\mathrm{T}_2])=\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{S}}^2$, where $\mathrm{T}_1$ and $\mathrm{T}_2$ are indeterminates; let $\mathrm{Y}$ be the ``zero section'' of this fibration. If $s$ is the unique closed point of $\mathrm{S}$, then $\mathrm{X}_s=\mathbf{V}_k^2$ and $\mathrm{Y}_s$ is the ``zero section'' of $\mathrm{X}_s$.
To see that the pair $(\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y})$ is not parafactorial, it suffices to show that the ring $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}_1,\mathrm{T}_2]_\mathfrak{m}$, where $\mathfrak{m}$ is the ideal $(\mathrm{T}_1)+(\mathrm{T}_2)$ which defines $\mathrm{Y}$, is not parafactorial \sref{IV.21.13.10}.
Let $\mathrm{B}_0=\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{red}}$, and let $\mathrm{U}$ and $\mathrm{U}_0$ be the complementary open subsets of the closed point in $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ and $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}_0)$; reasoning as in \sref{IV.21.13.9}, we have the exact sequence, extension of \sref{IV.21.13.9.1}
\[
  \Gamma(\mathrm{U},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})^*\to\Gamma(\mathrm{U}_0,\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0})^*\to\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U}_0,\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0})\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U}_0).
\]
Now, one has $\Gamma(\mathrm{U},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}})=\mathrm{B}$, $\Gamma(\mathrm{U}_0,\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0})=\mathrm{B}_0$ since $\operatorname{prof}(\mathrm{B})=\operatorname{prof}(\mathrm{B}_0)=2$ \sref{IV.5.10.5}.
On the other hand $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U}_0)=0$ since $\mathrm{B}_0$ is factorial, and $\mathrm{H}^1(\mathrm{U}_0,\sh{O}_{\mathrm{U}_0})\neq 0$ \cite[3, Example~III-1]{IV-41}; since the homomorphism $\mathrm{B}^*\to\mathrm{B}_0^*$ is surjective, we conclude that $\operatorname{Pic}(\mathrm{U})\neq 0$, hence that $\mathrm{B}$ is not parafactorial.
----- French corrections on page IV-4 327 -----

On est ainsi ramené au cas où $S=\operatorname{Spec}(A)$,
$X=\operatorname{Spec}(B)$, où $A$ est un anneau \emph{local}; puisque $X$ est
normal et $f$ lisse, $S$ est normal (17.5.7), donc $A$ est \emph{intègre} et
\emph{intégralement clos}. Considérons alors $A$ comme limite inductive de ses
\emph{sous-$\mathbb Z$-algèbres de type fini}; comme la clôture intégrale d'une telle
sous-$\mathbb Z$-algèbre est un sous-anneau de $A$ et est aussi une
$\mathbb Z$-algèbre \emph{de type fini} (7.8.3, (ii)

----- English, source_aligned/ega4-21.tex, page IV-4 329 -----
\oldpage[IV-4]{329}
(iv) The result of \sref{IV.21.14.3} was first proved by C.~Seshadri \cite{IV-44} in the particular case where $\mathrm{S}$ is an algebraic normal scheme over an algebraically closed field $k$ and $\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{S}\times_k\mathrm{T}$, where $\mathrm{T}$ is a $k$-algebraic prescheme smooth over $k$.
The proof of Seshadri \cite[pp.~188--189]{IV-44} is of global nature and uses the theory of Picard schemes.
It gives moreover (\emph{loc.\ cit.}) results such as the following (for which one does not currently possess a proof by local methods).
Let $\mathrm{S}$ and $\mathrm{T}$ be two preschemes locally of finite type over a field $k$, put $\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{S}\times_k\mathrm{T}$, and let $\mathrm{Z}$ be a $1$-codimensional cycle on $\mathrm{X}$ (regarded as an $\mathrm{S}$-prescheme). Suppose that the following conditions hold:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*\textsuperscript{o}]
  \item $\mathrm{S}$ and $\mathrm{T}$ are geometrically normal over $k$ \sref{IV.6.7.6};
  \item For every maximal point $\eta$ of $\mathrm{S}$, the $1$-codimensional cycle $\mathrm{Z}_\eta$ on the fibre $\mathrm{X}_\eta$, having the same multiplicity as $\mathrm{Z}$ at every point of $\mathrm{X}^{(1)}\cap\mathrm{X}_\eta$, is locally principal (in other words, it is the image of a divisor of $\mathrm{X}_\eta$, since $\mathrm{X}_\eta$ is normal);
  \item For all $s\in\mathrm{S}$, $\mathrm{Z}$ is principal at the maximal points of the fibre $\mathrm{X}_s$.
\end{enumerate}
Then $\mathrm{Z}$ is locally principal.
In other words, since $\mathrm{X}$ is normal \sref{IV.6.14.1}, for every $x\in\mathrm{X}$ which is not maximal in its fibre $\mathrm{X}_s$ and which belongs to none of the ``generic fibres'' $\mathrm{X}_\eta$ (which implies $\dim(\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x})\geq2$ by \sref{IV.6.1.1}), the local ring $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$, is parafactorial, by \sref{IV.21.13.15},~(b).\footnote{In the cited paper Seshadri in fact assumes that $k$ is algebraically closed and that $\mathrm{T}$ is separated and ``semi-complete'' (that is, $\Gamma(\mathrm{T},\sh{O}_{\mathrm{T}})\simeq k$), and replaces condition $3^{\mathrm{o}}$ by the stronger hypothesis that $\operatorname{Supp}(\mathrm{Z})$ contains no fibre $\mathrm{X}_s$, $s\in\mathrm{S}$. Since the statement is local on $\mathrm{S}$, however, condition $3^{\mathrm{o}}$ suffices. The resulting conclusion, interpreted as above in terms of parafactoriality of the rings $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X},x}$, is local on both $\mathrm{S}$ and $\mathrm{T}$; this removes altogether the assumptions that $\mathrm{T}$ is semi-complete and that $k$ is algebraically closed. Indeed, after first passing to an algebraic closure of $k$, one may assume that $\mathrm{T}$ is affine, embed it as an open subscheme of a normal projective prescheme over $k$, and apply Seshadri's result. This reduction also shows that it is enough to prove Seshadri's theorem when $\mathrm{T}$ is projective (rather than merely semi-complete), the case in which the Picard-scheme theory used by Seshadri is contained in chap.~VI of this Treatise.}.
\end{env}

\subsection{Relative divisors}
\label{subsection:IV.21.15}
\label{IV.21.15}

\begin{env}[21.15.1]
\label{IV.21.15.1}
Let $\mathrm{S}$ be a prescheme, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{S}$ a \emph{flat} morphism and locally of finite presentation.
We defined in \sref{IV.20.6.1} the sheaf of rings $\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S}}$ of the \emph{germs of meromorphic functions on $\mathrm{X}$ relative to $\mathrm{S}$}, sub-sheaf of $\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{X}}$; it is clear that the canonical injection $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{X}}$ \sref{IV.20.1.4.1} maps $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}$ into a sub-sheaf of $\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S}}$, with which we identify it.
Let $\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S}}^*$ be the sheaf (of multiplicative groups) of germs of invertible sections of $\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S}}$; it is therefore a sub-sheaf of $\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{X}}^*$ and contains $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}^*$ as a sub-sheaf.
\end{env}

\begin{definition}[21.15.2]
\label{IV.21.15.2}
We call \emph{sheaf of divisors on $\mathrm{X}$ relative to $\mathrm{S}$}, or \emph{sheaf of divisors on $\mathrm{X}$ transversal to $f$}, and denote $\mathscr{D}iv_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S}}$ the quotient sheaf (of commutative groups) $\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S}}^*/\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}}^*$; the sections of this sheaf over $\mathrm{X}$ are called \emph{divisors relative to $\mathrm{S}$}, or \emph{divisors on $\mathrm{X}$ transversal to $f$}; they form a commutative group denoted $\operatorname{Div}(\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S})$.
\end{definition}

It is clear that $\mathscr{D}iv_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S}}$ identifies canonically with a sub-sheaf of $\mathscr{D}iv_{\mathrm{X}}$, and hence $\operatorname{Div}(\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{S})$ with a subgroup of $\operatorname{Div}(\mathrm{X})$, which we denote also \emph{additively}.
----- French corrections on page IV-4 328 -----
[id 23969]

$A=\mathcal O_{S,s}$, $B=\mathcal O_{
